Figure 6:
QKD oracle model.
Honest sessions have access to the
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textsc{QKD-GET-KEY}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}},\cdot)
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textsc{QKD-GET-KEY-WITH-ID}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}},\cdot)
oracles (in
purple
),
while the adversary may query
QKD-KEY-HOLDERS
,
QKD-LEAK
, and
QKD-OVERRIDE
(in
red
).
The party calling
QKD-GET-KEY
specifies the key length
\ell
.

\mathit{kID}
is a (predictable) key identifier.
Besides the functionality, we add some
bookkeeping values (in green)
that are used in the proofs,
but are not available to either the honest user or the adversary.
Keys are removed in
Item

26
from the oracle when they are requested by the receiver (the party calling
QKD-GET-KEY-WITH-ID
).

We remark that
Honest
keys are uniformly random in the QKD oracle,
while real QKD keys have some negligibly small (but non-zero) statistical distance (
\varepsilon
)
to uniformly random keys.
We are already abstracting away many details of QKD,
and we expect that this additional simplification does not significantly impact the security of hybrid protocols.
For that reason we leave the proper modelling of
\varepsilon
-secure QKD as an open problem for future work.

Modelling QKD directly, including quantum and classical communication
[
MSU13
]
,
provides stronger security guarantees than modelling QKD
via an oracle as we do in this work.
Integration into an AKE protocol could then use the post-processing transcript
as an input to prevent attacks such as the Dependent-Key attack.
However, that would also requires that the AKE protocol has access to (the transcript of) QKD post-processing.
Instead the typical ETSI 014 interface (see
Section

2.5
) provides only a key and corresponding ID.

4.2
Modeling adversarial interaction

Secret-revealing oracles.
To account for the potential involvement of public-key algorithms, we assume—like in previous work on AKE—that public-key pairs, if such are used, have been distributed a priori. Given that we want our model to encompass hybrid QKD-PKC protocols, we want to be able to express security even upon failure of one of their components. We will model such failures by leakage of secret values like the involved parties’ long-term secret keys, or secret state information, which the attacker obtains via access to additional oracles:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\textsc{REVEAL}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}})
leaks the (real) session key
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sesKey}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]
to the adversary.
The model sets the flag
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revealed}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]
to record that this session key has been leaked.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\textsc{CORRUPT}}(i)
leaks the long-term secret key
\mathit{sk}_{i}
belonging to party
i
to the adversary.
The model sets the flag
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{corrupted}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]
to record that this party has been corrupted.
The adversary can still interact with corrupted parties (for example via
SEND-*
queries).

•

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\textsc{REVEAL-STATE}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}})
leaks the state
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{state}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]
to the adversary.
The model sets the flag
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revState}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]
to record that the state of this session has been leaked.

Malicious interaction with the QKD model is modelled via the following adversary oracle queries:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\textsc{QKD-LEAK}}(\mathit{kID})
leaks
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qKey}}}[\mathit{kID}]
to the adversary.
If the status was not already set (to
Corrupt
),
the model sets
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qStatus}}}[\mathit{kID}]:={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Revealed}}}
to record that the QKD key has been leaked,

•

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\textsc{QKD-OVERRIDE}}(\mathit{kID},k)
allows the adversary to overwrite
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qKey}}}[\mathit{kID}]:=k
,
The model sets
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qStatus}}}[\mathit{kID}]:={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Corrupt}}}
to record that the QKD key was set by the adversary.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\textsc{QKD-KEY-HOLDERS}}(\mathit{kID})
reveals to the adversary which sessions used the QKD key identified by
\mathit{kID}
.
This does not correspond any attack,
but rather reflects that we consider this information to be public.

The
QKD-LEAK
query corresponds to passive attacks on QKD
where the adversary learns the honestly generated key.
For example, this could reflect side-channel leakage from an honest execution of QKD.
The
QKD-OVERRIDE
query corresponds to active attacks on QKD,
where the adversary has some influence over the output that honest parties receive from the QKD device.
For example, this could reflect implementation errors, bad randomness, supply chain attacks,
or simply the fact that one of the executing QKD parties was corrupted.
Some of these attacks may only provide the adversary with some malleability of the output,
but letting the adversary choose these values freely only increases their power.

4.3
Session cleanness and security definition

If we would let the adversary call the above oracles without restrictions,
then no protocol can prevent the adversary from guessing
b
.
For example, revealing the test session key directly with
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\textsc{REVEAL}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*})

allows the adversary to compare directly against the output from the
TEST
query,
thereby revealing the value of
b
.
An attack that would work against
any
protocol is considered a trivial attack,
which we formalize using predicates, encoded as subroutines in
Fig.

8
.
The subroutine
TrivialPQC
defines what constitutes a trivial attack against the post-quantum cryptography in a protocol:
when the adversary executes an attack such that
TrivialPQC
does
not
hold,
then the protocol should be secure as long as the post-quantum primitives that it uses are secure.
The subroutine
TrivialQKD
defines what are considered (trivial) attacks against QKD:
when
TrivialQKD
does not hold,
a protocol should be secure if the used QKD key and other primitives used in the protocol are secure.
The notions are independent, such that a protocol should get security from both PQC and QKD
against attacks that are neither
TrivialPQC
nor
TrivialQKD
.
A session that is not trivially broken is also called
clean
:
we only require security for a clean test session.
To define cleanliness, we first require a precise definition of matching sessions.

Matching sessions.

Two sessions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{2}
are
matching
if

•

({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{1}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{1}])=({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{2}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{2}])
: they intend to communicate with each other,

•

({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sent}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{1}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{received}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{1}])=({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{received}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{2}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sent}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{2}])
: they have matching transcripts, and

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{1}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{2}]
: they have different roles.

We are only concerned with sessions that match with the test session.
The subroutine
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textsf{FindMatches}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*})
(defined in
Fig.

8
) creates
a set
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}
containing all sessions that match with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
.

TrivialPQC
.

The
TrivialPQC
notion is taken from
[
HKSU20
]
, with the exception that they inline
FindMatches
.

TrivialPQC
excludes certain combinations of queries because no PQC protocol can protect against them,
as indicated by the attacks below.

1.

If the test session did not accept, then
the session key
\bot
is trivially distinguished from random.

2.

If the test session was revealed, then
the session key is known to the adversary and easily distinguished from random.

3.

If the long-term key and state of the test session were all leaked, then
all secret values used to compute the test session key are known to the adversary.

4.

If there is no matching session and the peer has been corrupted,
then the test session may have been executed directly with the adversary.

5.

If there is a matching session, that session may not be revealed.

6.

If there is a matching session, that session needs to have at least one secret value
(long-term key or state) so the adversary cannot recompute the session key.

In addition to the above list,
if the adversary manages to create more than one session that matches with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
,
then the attack is considered non-trivial.
Note that this holds even if the adversary reveals (one of) those matching sessions,
so effectively an adversary that can create multiple matching sessions can always win the game.
This check formalizes a requirement of the CK
+
model
[
Kra05
]
,
which only considers protocols where the initial message is unique,
while allowing us to quantify the security loss induced by lack of randomness in the protocol.
Intuitively this check ensures that in a secure protocol the adversary cannot create multiple sessions that output the same key,
which might lead to confusion attacks, for example if the adversary swaps different messages encrypted under the same key.
The above is formally encoded by subroutine
TrivialPQC
in
Fig.

8
.

TrivialQKD
.

We can only expect security from QKD if the test session used at least one
Honest
QKD key.
Formally, we require that there exists a
\mathit{kID}
such that

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qStatus}}}[\mathit{kID}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Honest}}}
, and

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qSentSid}}}[\mathit{kID}]
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[\mathit{kID}]
.

We cannot guarantee security if the adversary revealed the test session or the matching session.
However, we also want to reward the adversary if they can create multiple
completed
matching sessions,
but only if the test session used an honest QKD key
(otherwise a trivial adversary against QKD exists that can create multiple accepting sessions
by setting the QKD key with
QKD-OVERRIDE
and delivering the key to multiple recipients).

The above is encoded by the subroutine
TrivialQKD
in
Fig.

8
.
Note that if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qStatus}}}[\mathit{kID}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Honest}}}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[\mathit{kID}]
is equivalent to
[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[\mathit{kID}]
,
because
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[\mathit{kID}]
has length at most one,
since
Honest
keys are deleted after they are delivered once.
Also note that if the adversary manages to create multiple accepting matching sessions
(without breaking all QKD keys used),
then they are allowed to reveal those sessions and can therefore win the game.

In addition to the above, we also require that the test session
accepted,
it was not revealed
and if there is one matching session
then that can also not be revealed.

AttackPQC
.

Our protocol is vulnerable to an attack:
the adversary can impersonate the responder without compromising the responder long-term key,
instead they reveal the initiator state to reveal
k_{b}
.
If the adversary also breaks QKD, they can complete the responder session themselves.
This is a known attack on the classical protocol (without QKD)
[
HKSU20
]
,
and it is not considered a trivial attack
because there exist protocols that do protect against this scenario.
Our work aims to integrate QKD into an existing AKE protocol,
not to fix the classical protocol.
Note that QKD does help:
the adversary must break QKD to exploit this attack.
This attack is formally encoded by the subroutine
AttackPQC
in
Fig.

8
.

Formal definition.

Figure

7
ties everything together and formalizes the above model.
The three games
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}
,
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}
, and
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}

only differ by which cleanliness/trivial predicate they use.
The games are defined relative to a challenge bit
b
,
indicating if the adversary will receive a real or a random key.
The challenger generates long-term public keys for each party
and initializes the QKD oracle,
then adversary receives the public keys and gets to interact with the parties
and their secrets via the earlier defined oracles.
The adversary then makes a guess
b^{\prime}
for the challenge bit,
which the game outputs if the adversary is considered non-trivial
(and otherwise the game outputs 0).
The adversary is said to
win
the game if the game output equals
b
.

GAME

\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{b}(\Pi,A)

01

\mathit{sID}_{\mathit{ctr}}:=0

02

\mathit{sID}^{*}:=0

03

\textbf{for }i\in\set{1,\dots,\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}}

04

\quad(\mathit{pk}_{i},\mathit{sk}_{i})\leftarrow\textsf{KeyGen}_{\mathit{stat}}()

05

\textsf{QkdInit}()

06

b^{\prime}\leftarrow A^{\mathcal{O}}(\mathit{pk}_{1},\dots,\mathit{pk}_{\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}})

07

\mathit{matches}:=\textsf{FindMatches}(\mathit{sID}^{*})

08

\textbf{if }\textsf{Trivial}(\mathit{matches})

09

\quad\textbf{return }0

\sslash
adversary loses

10

\textbf{return }b^{\prime}

GAME

\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{b}(\Pi,A)

11

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{ctr}}:=0

12

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}:=0

13

\textbf{for }i\in\set{1,\dots,\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}}

14

\quad(\mathit{pk}_{i},\mathit{sk}_{i})\leftarrow\textsf{KeyGen}_{\mathit{stat}}()

15

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textsf{QkdInit}}()

16

b^{\prime}\leftarrow A^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathcal{O}}(\mathit{pk}_{1},\dots,\mathit{pk}_{\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}})

17

Figure 8:

A protocol is considered trivially broken if too much information is revealed,
such that no expectation of security remains for
any
protocol.

Correctness definition.

We require the protocol to be correct,
which intuitively means
that both participating parties will compute the same session key
in the presence of a passive adversary.
In the AKE literature
[
Kra05
,
FSXY12
]
this requirement is usually formalized by requiring that
if two parties complete matching sessions, then they compute the same session key.
Since we allow an active adversary to change QKD keys via the
QKD-OVERRIDE
query,
we also need to exclude that capability to formally define what we mean by a passive adversary.
We enforce this by requiring that all QKD keys used by the matching sessions are not
Corrupt
.

Definition 5
(AKE-correctness)

An AKE protocol
\Pi
is
\delta
-correct
if the following holds.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}
and
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}
denote two sessions such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sesKey}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]\neq\bot
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sesKey}}}[\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}]\neq\bot
(they are complete),

\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textsf{FindMatches}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}})
(they are matching), and
for all
\mathit{kID}
such that
\mathit{kID}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qSentSid}}}
or
\mathit{kID}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}

it holds that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qStatus}}}[\mathit{kID}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Corrupt}}}
(QKD was not tampered with),
then

\Pr[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sesKey}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sesKey}}}[\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}]]\leq\delta.

We emphasize that both sessions need to be completed.
Without this requirement,
an adversary might duplicate a QKD key ID
such that multiple honest sessions will request the QKD key using
QKD-GET-KEY-WITH-ID
,
but only one will succeed.
We consider this to be an active attack and therefore exclude it from the correctness definition.

Security definition.

For non-
TrivialPQC
attacks, we use one of the two existing notions in our security definition:
the stronger notion of “key
IND
istinguishability against
A
ctive
A
ttacks against
PQC
”
can be defined using the
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{AA}
-
\mathsf{PQC}
game,
and the weaker notion of “key
IND
istinguishability against
A
ctive
A
ttacks
against
PQC
without
St
ate reveal in the test session”
is defined using the
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{StAA}
-
\mathsf{PQC}
game.
We include both games in
Fig.

7
for completeness,
but we only use the (weaker)
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{StAA}
-
\mathsf{PQC}
game in our analysis of the AKE protocol of
Fig.

4
.
The difference from
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{StAA}
-
\mathsf{PQC}
is that it calls
TrivialPQC
in
Item

08
instead of
AttackPQC
in
Item

18
.

Definition 6
(StAA-PQC Advantage)

Define the game
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{StAA}
-
\mathsf{PQC}
b
for
b\in\{0,1\}

as in
Figs.

7
and
8
.
The
advantage
of an adversary
A
against an AKE protocol
\Pi
is

\mathrm{Adv}_{\Pi}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}}(A)=\big|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]\big|\,.

Against non-
TrivialQKD
attacks, we define a new security notion:
“key
IND
istinguishability against
A
ctive
A
ttacks against
QKD
”,
for which we use the
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}
game.

Definition 7
(AA-QKD Advantage)

Define the game
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{AA}
-
\mathsf{QKD}
b
for
b\in\{0,1\}

as in
Figs.

7
and
8
.
The
advantage
of an adversary
A
against an AKE protocol
\Pi
is

\mathrm{Adv}_{\Pi}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}}(A)=\big|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]\big|\,.

formally describes our authenticated key exchange protocol with a QKD oracle from

t:=(m_{1},(c_{a},c_{e},\mathit{kID}))

(k_{\mathit{sess}},\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\textsf{Combine}(i,j,t,k_{\mathit{qkd}},k_{\mathit{pqc}})

25

\textbf{if }\tau_{1}\neq\tau_{1}^{\prime}\textbf{ or }\tau_{2}\neq\tau_{2}^{\prime}:\textbf{abort}

26

\textbf{return }k_{\mathit{sess}}

\textsf{Combine}(i_{I},i_{R},t,k_{\mathit{qkd}},k_{\mathit{pqc}})

27

(k_{\mathit{qkd},m}\mathbin{\|}k_{\mathit{qkd},s}):=k_{\mathit{qkd}}

28

(k_{\mathit{pqc},m}\mathbin{\|}k_{\mathit{pqc},s}):=k_{\mathit{pqc}}

29

k_{\mathit{sess}}:=k_{\mathit{qkd},s}\oplus k_{\mathit{pqc},s}

30

\tau_{1}:=\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}_{k_{\mathit{qkd},m}}((t,i_{I},i_{R}))

31

\tau_{2}:=\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}_{k_{\mathit{pqc},m}}((t,\tau_{1},i_{I},i_{R}))

32

\textbf{return }(k_{\mathit{sess}},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

Figure 9:

A formal implementation of our AKE protocol
\Pi=(\textsf{Init},\textsf{SendM1},\textsf{SendM2})
,
using the QKD oracle
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textsc{QKD-GET-KEY}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textsc{QKD-GET-KEY-WITH-ID}})
,
in the case of end-to-end QKD.

We use the notation
(x\mathbin{\|}y)
to split keys into their required lengths:
in all protocols the first part becomes a
MAC
key,
so
x
should have the length of a
MAC
key and
y
should be the remaining bits.
We use destructuring assignments to “parse” the incoming messages and state,
this should be interpreted as a shorthand notation for
checking if the parsed value has the correct format and aborting the session if it has not.

Our protocol uses two
MAC
s,
the inner
\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}
uses a key generated by QKD,
and we require statistical security:
strong unforgeability (see
Definition

4
) must hold against computationally unbounded adversaries.
Indeed such
MAC
s exist
[
WC81
]

and are usually canonical,
but a non-canonical
MAC
would suffice.
The outer
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
uses a key generated by PQC primitives,
this
MAC
must either have statistical security,
or it must be canonical (in which case computational security suffices).
This property protects against a (potentially unbounded) adversary
that changes a matching session into a non-matching one
by modifying the outer tag exchanged between two honest parties:
both statistically secure and canonical
MAC
s will reject such modifications.
For simplicity, we assume both
MAC
s are canonical.

Note that the order of nesting
MAC
s is important.
Consider the alternative protocol that is identical to
Fig.

9
,
but computes
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
first and
\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}
second:
this protocol is vulnerable to the following attack.
An adversary can query
QKD-OVERRIDE
to change
k_{\mathit{qkd}}
without changing
\mathit{kID}
.
This modification of the key does not change the transcript between honest users,
so that
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
does not detect anything,
yet the honest parties will compute
\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}
with a different key
(so that the sent tag almost certainly differs from the one that is accepted).
The adversary (who knows
k_{\mathit{qkd}}
) can swap out the QKD tag,
so that both parties accept with the same session key,
but their sessions are non-matching,
and thus the adversary may reveal the peer session key.

This attack does not apply to
Fig.

9
,
because the adversary cannot change
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
without changing the transcript.
The reason is that
\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}
authenticates the full transcript,
and by correctness of the
KEM
s,
both honest parties will (almost certainly) compute the same
k_{\mathit{pqc}}

and thus the sent tag is the only one that is accepted.

First we prove that the protocol
\Pi
defined in
Fig.

9

is correct when implemented with correct components.

Theorem 5.1
(AKE-correctness)

Let
\Pi
be the AKE protocol defined in
Fig.

9
,
where
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}
is a
\delta_{\mathit{eph}}
-correct KEM
and
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}
is a
\delta_{\mathit{stat}}
-correct KEM.
Then
\Pi
is a
(\delta_{\mathit{eph}}+2\delta_{\mathit{stat}})
-correct AKE.

Proof

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}
and
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}
be complete matching sessions with uncorrupted QKD keys,
according to
Definition

5
.
Since the sessions match, one of them is an initiator (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}
),
the other is a responder (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{resp}}
)
and the transcript contains a single QKD key ID (
\mathit{kID}^{*}
).
Because
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}
completed it received a QKD key:
k_{\mathit{qkd}}:={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textsc{QKD-GET-KEY-WITH-ID}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{resp}},\mathit{kID}^{*})\neq\bot
,
and since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qStatus}}}[\mathit{kID}^{*}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Corrupt}}}
, both sessions received the same QKD key.

For a matching session, the only other source of error is a decapsulation failure.
The protocol contains three decapsulations,
and so the probability of any failure is at most the sum of the probability
that either one of them fails, so that

\Pr[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sesKey}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sesKey}}}[\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}]]\leq\delta_{\mathit{eph}}+2\delta_{\mathit{stat}}.

∎

We now give a bound on the advantage that any (computationally bounded) adversary has on
protocol
\Pi
(defined in
Fig.

9
)
based on the advantage on its secure PQC components.

Theorem 5.2
(PQC-based security)

Let
\Pi
be the protocol of
Fig.

9
,
where
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}
is
\delta_{\mathit{eph}}
-correct and has key length
\ell_{\texttt{eph}}
,
and
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}
is
\delta_{\mathit{stat}}
-correct,
has collision probabilities
\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})
and
\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})
,
and has key length
\ell_{\texttt{stat}}
.
Let
A
be an
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}
adversary executing
\Pi
with
\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}
parties
that establishes
\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}
sessions that makes at most
q_{H}
calls
to the key-derivation function
KDF
(modelled as a QROM).
Then there exist

\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CPA}
adversary
B_{1}
against
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}
,

\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CPA}
adversary
B_{2}
against
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}
,

\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CCA}
adversary
B_{3}
against
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}
, and

\mathsf{OT}
-
\mathsf{sEUF}
-
\mathsf{CMA}
adversary
B_{4}
against
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Adv}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}}(\Pi,A)\leq

\displaystyle\phantom{0}\mathbin{\hphantom{+}}2\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\left(\delta_{\mathit{eph}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CPA}}(B_{1})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{0}+2\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\left(\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{2})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}\right)+2\cdot\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})

\displaystyle\phantom{0}+8\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\left(\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{3})+\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})+\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\frac{2q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}+\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{4})\right)

where the runtime of
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4}
is about that of
A
.

Readers familiar with the KEM-TLS protocol and its security proof
[
SSW20
]
,
might be surprised to see that
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CPA}
security suffices for
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}
in our protocol.
The reason our protocol does not require
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{1}
\mathsf{CCA}
(a one-time variant of
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CCA}
security)
is that an initiator session
immediately
aborts when an adversary modifies or replaces the ephemeral encapsulation,
because they cannot accompany that same message with the correct
MAC
tags.

Proof
(PQC-based security)

We split the proof into two cases.
The first case,
Section

5
, concerns the case
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset
.
For the second case (
Section

5.1
), we look at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}=\emptyset
.

Section

5
: passive attack

is formally treated in
Section

5.1
.
In this case, a matching session exists.

We will prove that with overwhelming probability, this matching session must be unique.
Since we are assuming that there are matching sessions,
and since the responder session does not have a state,
there is no benefit to the attacker in revealing the state of
the responder. Therefore, assuming there is exactly one matching session,
we can always assume that the
state of the responder session is not revealed.

We will split
Section

5
into two sub-cases:

5.1

The state of the initiator has not been revealed.
Thus, the ephemeral secret key
\mathit{sk}_{e}
has not been revealed to the attacker,
and we base security of the final session key on the security of the ephemeral KEM.

5.1

The initiator has not been corrupted.
Thus, the initiator’s static key
\mathit{sk}_{i}
has not been revealed to the attacker.
We base security of the final session key on the security of the initiator static
KEM.

Lemma 2
(Case 1)

There exist adversaries
B_{1},B_{2}
such that

\big|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]\big|\\
\leq 2\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\cdot\left(\delta_{\mathit{eph}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CPA}}(B_{1})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}}}+\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\left(\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{2})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}\right)\right)\\
+2\cdot\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})

and the runtime of
B_{1},B_{2}
is about that of
A
.

Section

5.1
: active attack

is formally treated in
Section

5.2
. This means that no matching session exists.
Since we only consider non-trivial attacks, we in particular rule out that the test session’s peer has been corrupted.
It is thus safe to assume that their static key has not been revealed, and we base security of
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
on the security of the peer’s static
KEM.
It remains to consider the case that there is a non-matching session that computes the same
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
,
for example if the adversary swapped out
\mathit{kID}
or changes one of the
MAC
tags.
However, then by the unforgeability of
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
,
this will be detected by the initiator who will then abort.
That means there is only one accepting (responder) session whose session key depends on
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
,
and thus
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
is independent from the adversary view.

Lemma 3
(Case 2)

There exist adversaries
B_{3},B_{4}
such that

\big|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}=\emptyset]\\
-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}=\emptyset]\big|\\
\leq 8\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\Bigg(\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{3})+\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})+\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\frac{2q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}+\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{4})\Bigg)

and the runtime of
B_{3},B_{4}
is about that of
A
.

In order to bound

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]\right|\\

we combine
Lemma

2
and
Lemma

3
by taking the sum of the bounds.
This gives

\displaystyle|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]|

\displaystyle\phantom{0}\mathbin{\hphantom{+}}2\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\left(\delta_{\mathit{eph}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CPA}}(B_{1})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{0}+2\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\left(\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{2})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}\right)+2\cdot\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})

\displaystyle\phantom{0}+8\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\left(\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{3})+\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})+\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\frac{2q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}+\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{4})\right).

The proofs of the
Lemmas

2
and
3
can be found in the following sections.

We now give a bound on the advantage that any (potentially unbounded) adversary has on
protocol
\Pi
(defined in
Fig.

9
)
based on the advantage on its secure QKD components.

Theorem 5.3
(QKD-based security)

Let
\Pi
be the protocol of
Fig.

9
,
where
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}
is
\delta_{\mathit{eph}}
-correct
and
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}
is
\delta_{\mathit{stat}}
-correct.
Let
A
be an
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}
adversary executing
\Pi

that establishes
\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}
sessions.
Then there exist and
\mathsf{OT}
-
\mathsf{sEUF}
-
\mathsf{CMA}
adversary
B_{7}
against
\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Adv}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}}(\Pi,A)\leq 2\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\left(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}(2\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\delta_{\mathit{eph}})+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{7})\right)

where the runtime of
B_{7}
is potentially unbounded.

Proof sketch (QKD-based security).

The formal proof for
Theorem

5.3
is given in
Section

5.3
.
The idea is that the QKD key has not leaked to (and has not been set by) the adversary.
Our QKD oracle guarantees that honest QKD keys can be handed out at most twice.
Thus, we focus on the test session and the session that uses the same QKD key.
In our protocol
\Pi
, this will always be an initiator/responder pair.
We base security of the final session key on the MAC,
this ensures that the responder session is a matching session, or the initiator will abort.
In both cases, the other session’s session key cannot be revealed.
Thus, the QKD key (and thus the session key) looks uniformly random to the adversary.

5.1
Security proof for
Section

5
: passive attack,
Lemma

2

Formally, we want to upper bound

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]\right|\kern 5.0pt,

which we will do via a sequence of games.

In the following section, we will first switch to game
G_{1}
to ensure we are in the correct case,
after that the hop to game
G_{2}
ensures that there is precisely one matching session.
Game
G_{3}
makes a guess which two sessions will be the initiator and responder,
so that in any game hereafter we can talk about ‘the’ initiator and responder sessions.

After that, we split the rest of the proof into two subcases:

Section

5.1
where the ephemeral secret key of the initiator session has not been revealed and

Section

5.1
where the static secret key of initiator has not been revealed.

GAME

16

\quad\textbf{return }0

\sslash
G_{3,b}

\textbf{return }b^{\prime}

Figure 10:
G_{0,b}
-
G_{3,b}
. Any oracle unchanged is not included.

Games
G_{0,b}
For each challenge bit
b\in\{0,1\}
, game
G_{0,b}
is the original game
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{StAA}
-
\mathsf{PQC}
{}_{b}(\Pi,A)
, so

\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{b}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]=\Pr[G_{0,b}\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset].

Games
G_{1,b}

In this game, we add the check that there is at least one matching session in
Item

10
,
to ensure we are in the right scenario.
For each challenge bit
b\in\{0,1\}
, the new game
G_{1,b}
only differs from
G_{0,b}
when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}=\emptyset
.
We’re only considering the case where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset
so we have

\Pr[G_{0,b}\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]=\Pr[G_{1,b}\Rightarrow 1]\kern 5.0pt.

Games
G_{2,b}
For each challenge bit
b\in\{0,1\}
, we introduce a new game
G_{2,b}
that returns 0 in
Item

11
if there is more than one matching session.
Since
G_{1,b}
and
G_{2,b}
only differ upon this case, we have

\left|\Pr[G_{1,b}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{2,b}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq\Pr[\text{Return in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{line:abort}}]\kern 5.0pt.

We now bound
\Pr[\text{Return in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{line:abort}}]
for each of the two possible roles the test session could have.
First, assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Initiator}}}
.
Having multiple matching sessions of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
means that there are two distinct
Responder
sessions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{2}
that sent the same first response.
Formally, this means
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sent}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{1}]=[c_{a},c_{e},\mathit{kID},\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sent}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{2}]
.
Since all ciphertexts were generated honestly, the probability that
both the static and ephemeral ciphertexts are equal for both sessions is at most

\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{eph}})
(see
Definition

2
).
Since there are at most
(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-1)
possible sessions that could create this situation and since
\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{eph}})\leq 1
, we have

\Pr[\text{Return in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{line:abort}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Initiator}}}]\leq(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-1)\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{eph}})\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})\\
\leq\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}}).

Now consider the case that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Responder}}}
.
Having multiple matching sessions of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
means there are two distinct
Initiator
sessions

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{2}
which sent the same initial message.
Formally, this means
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sent}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{1}]=[c_{b},\mathit{pk}_{e}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sent}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{2}]
.
This means that both sessions need to generate the same ciphertext to Bob’s static key,
which happens with probability at most
\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})
. Similarly, the probability that
both sessions generated the same public key
\mathit{pk}_{e}
is at most
\mu(\textsf{KeyGen}_{\mathit{eph}})
(see
Definition

2
).
Since there are at most
(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-1)
sessions that could be matching, we have

\Pr[\text{Return in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{line:abort}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Responder}}}]\leq(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-1)\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})\cdot\mu(\textsf{KeyGen}_{\mathit{eph}})\\
\leq\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})\kern 5.0pt.

In total, this gives

\Pr[\text{Return in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{line:abort}}]\leq\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})\kern 5.0pt.

So far, we have

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]\right|

\leq\left|\Pr[G_{2,1}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{2,0}\Rightarrow 1]\right|+2\cdot\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})\kern 5.0pt.

If games
G_{0,b}-G_{2,b}
do not return early, we can safely assume that
|{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}|=1
.
In other words, there is exactly one matching session of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
, which we will denote by
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}
.

Games
G_{3,b}

At the start of both games
G_{3,b}
, we guess the session IDs of the init and responder sessions
in
Item

03
and
Item

04
,
and we check the guesses are correct in
Item

14
to
Item

16
.
If the session IDs are guessed correctly,
G_{2,b}
and
G_{3,b}
are equal,
and if one of the sessions IDs has not been guessed correctly,
G_{3,b}
outputs
0
.
Therefore

\Pr[G_{2,b}\Rightarrow 1]=\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\cdot\Pr[G_{3,b}\Rightarrow 1]

from which we get

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]\right|\\
\leq\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\cdot\left|\Pr[G_{3,1}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{3,0}\Rightarrow 1]\right|+2\cdot\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})\kern 5.0pt.

(1)

If the attacker does any action that makes it so that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textsf{AttackPQC}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}})
returns
true
, he will not be able
to distinguish anything. Therefore it is safe to assume that
the attacker’s actions do not cause
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textsf{AttackPQC}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}})
to
return
true
.
In the case where
|{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}|=1
, and we name the matching session
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}
,
this comes down to:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sesKey}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]\neq\bot
(the session actually finished)

2.

\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revealed}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]
(the test session’s key was not revealed)

3.

\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{corrupted}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]]
or
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revState}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]
(either the test session’s owner was not corrupted or the test session was not revealed)

4.

\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revealed}}}[\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}]
(the matching session’s key is not revealed)

5.

\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{corrupted}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}]]
or
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revState}}}[\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}]
(either the matching session’s owner was not corrupted or the matching session was not revealed)

Since the responder session has no state,
we may assume
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revState}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{resp}}]
.
And since
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}},\allowbreak{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{resp}}\}=\set{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}}
,
the conjuction of properties
3
and
5

is equivalent to
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{corrupted}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}]]\lor\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revState}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}]
.
Knowing this, we have to prove the security
of the protocol in the following case:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sesKey}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]\neq\bot
,
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revealed}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]
,

\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revealed}}}[\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}]
, and either
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{corrupted}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}]

or
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revState}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}]
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}}\}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Initiator}}}
.

We now look at two possible cases:

•

Section

5.1
: where we assume that the state of the initiator has not been revealed.

•

Section

5.1
: where we assume that the initiator has not been corrupted.

In
Section

5.1
we assume that
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revState}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}]
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}
is the session id of the initiator.
We denote this case with
\neg\text{st}
.
Since the state of the initiator is not revealed, we can assume that with overwhelming probability,
the adversary does not know the secret key
\mathit{sk}_{e}
and therefore also not
k_{e}
.
Since
k_{e}
is always part of the input of the
KDF
and since the
KDF
is modelled as a random
oracle,
the resulting session key is secure.

In
Section

5.1
we assume that
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{corrupted}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}]
,
where again
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}
is the session id of the initiator.
We denote this case with
\neg sk(i)
.
Since the long-term secret key of the initiator is not known to the attacker,
we can assume that with overwhelming probability they do not know
k_{a}
.
And again, since
k_{a}
is part of the input of the
KDF
,
the resulting session key is secure.

: State not revealed

Games
G_{3,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
-
G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
are defined in
Fig.

11
.

Games
G_{3,b}
and
G_{3,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
are the same for both
b
, so

\left|\Pr[G_{3,1}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{st}]-\Pr[G_{3,0}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{st}]\right|=\left|\Pr[G_{3,1}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{st}]-\Pr[G_{3,0}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{st}]\right|.

Games
G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{st}}

We add the condition
\neg\text{st}
in game
G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
at
Item

17
, to ensure we are in the correct case. Since

\Pr[G_{3,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{st}]=\Pr[G_{4,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]

for both
b
, which gives

\left|\Pr[G_{3,1}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{st}]-\Pr[G_{3,0}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{st}]\right|=\left|\Pr[G_{4,1}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{4,0}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|.

Games
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{st}}

For
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
we move the calls to
\textsf{KeyGen}_{\mathit{eph}}
and
\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{eph}}
of the
ephemeral
KEM
to the game itself,
Items

08
and
09
.
These values then replace the ephemeral public key, secret key, key and ciphertext in
Items

27
,
40
and
64
.
This change is only noticed by the distinguisher
if
\textsf{Decaps}_{\mathit{eph}}
returns the wrong key.
Since the probability that this happens is at most
\delta_{\mathit{eph}}
(see
Definition

1
),
we have

\left|\Pr[G_{4,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{5,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq\delta_{\mathit{eph}}.

(2)

Games
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{st}}

In this game, we replace the ephemeral key of the test sessions generated by
\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{eph}}

with a uniformly random key, in
Item

10
.
To justify replacing the ephemeral key
k_{e}
with a uniformly random key,
we now relate the potential additional advantage to
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CPA}
security of the ephemeral
KEM
.
We do this by showing that we can use any adversary
C
that distinguishes between
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{st}}

and
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
to create an adversary
B_{1}
of the ephemeral
KEM
that has
a runtime about that of
C
.
The adversary
C
gets access to either
G_{5,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}
or
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
and then has
to output either
0
or
1
.
In the
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CPA}
game, the input to
B_{1}
consists of
\mathit{pk}
,
c
and
k_{i}
,
where
\mathit{pk}
and
c
are generated by the
\textsf{KeyGen}_{\mathit{eph}}
and
\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{eph}}
respectively,
and
k_{i}
is generated by
\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{eph}}
if
i=0
, and uniformly random if
i=1
.
This attacker
B_{1}
can use the adversary
C
as follows:
replace the
\widetilde{\mathit{pk}_{e}}
,
\widetilde{c_{e}}
and
\widetilde{k_{e}}

after
Item

10
with the inputs of
B_{1}
. Since these values were generated with
the same algorithms, this does not change their distribution.
Now
C
, with access to these algorithms, outputs
0
or
1
.
Let
B_{1}
then give the same output as
C
.

The difference between
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
and
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
is whether
\widetilde{k_{e}}
is
generated by
\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{eph}}
or uniformly random.
Since
C
distinguishes between the honestly generated key and a uniformly random key,
replacing this key with the key
k_{i}
for which we have the same question
allows distinguishing an honestly generated key from a uniformly random key for
the
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CPA}
game.
Since this is true for all adversaries
C
, we have

\left|\Pr[G_{5,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{6,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CPA}}(B_{1})\kern 5.0pt.

(3)

Games
G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{st}}

Here, we replace the output of the
KDF
of the test session with a uniformly random
value. The value is generated at
Item

11
and used at
Items

43
and
67
.

Since we model
\textsf{KDF}:\{0,1\}^{2\ell_{\texttt{stat}}+\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}}
as a random oracle, we can use the one-way to hiding
Lemma

1

to bound the distance between
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
and
G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
.
Define
H(\cdot)=\textsf{KDF}(k_{a},k_{b},\cdot)
where
k_{a}
and
k_{b}
are the
keys used in session
s_{\mathit{init}}
.
Then for any fixed
k_{a}
,
k_{b}
we can replace sampling

\textsf{KDF}\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{2\ell_{\texttt{stat}}+\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})
with sampling

H\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})
.
Let
B^{H}(x,y)
be the execution of
G_{6,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}

where we replace
k_{e}
of the test session with
x
,
and
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
of the test session with
y
.
Then

\Pr[G_{6,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[b^{\prime}=1:b^{\prime}\leftarrow B^{H}(x,H(x))]

and

\Pr[G_{7,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[b^{\prime}=1:b^{\prime}\leftarrow B^{H}(x,y),y\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}}].

where we also take the average over

H\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})

and
x\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}
.
As in
Lemma

1
, let
C
be an oracle algorithm that on input
x
does the following: pick

i\leftarrow_{\$}\{1,...,q_{h}\}
and
y\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{m}
, run
B^{H}(x,y)
until just before
the
i
-th query, measure the argument of the query in the computational basis,
and output the measurement outcome.
We define

P_{C}=\Pr[x=x^{\prime}:x^{\prime}\leftarrow C^{H}(x)]

where the probability is also over

H\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})
and
x\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}
.
But since
x
is not available to
C
at any point, the best the attacker can do is guess,
which means that
P_{C}=\frac{1}{2^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}}
.

Then by
Lemma

1
we have

\left|\Pr[G_{6,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{7,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq 2q_{H}\sqrt{P_{C}}=2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}}}.

(4)

Next, we want to bound

\left|\Pr[G_{7,0}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{7,1}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|.

Key
k_{0}^{*}
is generated as a part
k_{\mathit{pqc}}\oplus k_{\mathit{qkd}}
, where
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
is uniformly random.
Furthermore, this key is only used in the matching session of the test session, which
the adversary is not allowed to reveal.
Key
k_{1}^{*}
is generated uniformly random. Therefore the adversary cannot distinguish between

G_{7,0}^{\neg\text{st}}
and
G_{7,1}^{\neg\text{st}}
, and we have

\Pr[G_{7,0}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{7,1}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1].

(5)

Putting equations (
2
), (
3
) and (
4
) together, we get that

\left|\Pr[G_{4,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{7,b}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq\delta_{\mathit{eph}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CPA}}(B_{1})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}}}.

Combining this with (
5
), we get that

\left|\Pr[G_{3,0}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\wedge\neg st\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{3,1}^{{\neg\text{st}}}\wedge\neg st\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq 2\delta_{\mathit{eph}}+2\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CPA}}(B_{1})+4q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}}}.

(6)

G_{3,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}

Games
G_{3,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
-
G_{8,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
are defined in
Fig.

14
.

Games
G_{3,b}
and
G_{3,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
are the same for both
b
, so

\left|\Pr[G_{3,1}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{sk}(i)]-\Pr[G_{3,0}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{sk}(i)]\right|\\
=\left|\Pr[G_{3,1}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{sk}(i)]-\Pr[G_{3,0}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{sk}(i)]\right|.

Games
G_{4,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}

We add the condition
\neg\text{sk}(i)
in game
G_{4,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
at
Item

23
, to ensure we are in the correct case. Since

\Pr[G_{3,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{sk}(i)]=\Pr[G_{4,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]

for both
b
, which gives

\left|\Pr[G_{3,1}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{sk}(i)]-\Pr[G_{3,0}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1\wedge\neg\text{sk}(i)]\right|=\left|\Pr[G_{4,1}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{4,0}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|.

(7)

Games
G_{5,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}

For
G_{5,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
we guess which party is the owner of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}
.
In line
05
we add the guess for the owner of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\text{init}}
,
and in line
21
we check if it is correct.
If the party is guessed incorrectly, the game returns
0
.
If the party is guessed correctly, the game returns
1
if and only if

G_{4,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
would have returned
1
.
Since the party is guessed uniformly at random, we get

\Pr[G_{4,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]=\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\cdot\Pr[G_{5,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]\kern 5.0pt.

(8)

Games
G_{6,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}

For
G_{6,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
we move the call
\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}}
of the
static
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}
to the
EST
oracle,
Item

08
.
These values then replace the static key and ciphertext in
Items

40
and
64
.
This change is only noticed by the distinguisher
if
\textsf{Decaps}_{\mathit{stat}}
returns the wrong key.
Since the probability of returning the wrong key is at most
\delta_{\mathit{stat}}
(see
Definition

1
),
we have

\left|\Pr[G_{5,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{6,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq\delta_{\mathit{stat}}.

(9)

Games
G_{7,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}

In this game, we replace Bob’s static key of the test sessions generated by
\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}}

with a uniformly random key, in
Item

10
.
To justify replacing the static key
k_{b}
with a uniformly random key,
we now relate the potential additional advantage to
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CCA}
security of the static
KEM
.
We do this by showing that we can use any adversary
C
that distinguishes between
G_{5,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}

and
G_{6,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
to create an adversary
B_{2}
of the static
KEM
that has
a runtime about that of
C
.
The adversary
C
gets access to either
G_{5,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
or
G_{6,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
and then has
to output either
0
or
1
.
In the
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}
game, the input to
B_{2}
consists of
\mathit{pk}
,
c
and
k_{i}
,
where
\mathit{pk}
and
c
are generated by the
\textsf{KeyGen}_{\mathit{stat}}
and
\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}}
respectively,
and
k_{i}
is generated by
\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}}
if
i=0
, and uniformly random if
i=1
.
This attacker
B_{2}
can use the distinguisher
C
as follows:
replace the
\widetilde{\mathit{pk}_{b}}
,
\widetilde{c_{b}}
and
\widetilde{k_{b}}

after
Item

10
with the inputs of
B_{2}
. Since these values were generated with
the same algorithms, this does not change their distribution.
Furthermore, any call to
\textsf{Decaps}_{\mathit{stat}}
using key
\widetilde{k}_{b}
gets replaced by
a call to the
Decaps
oracle of the
\mathsf{IND}-\mathsf{CCA}
game.
Now
C
, with access to these algorithms, outputs
0
or
1
.
Let
B_{2}
then give the same output as
C
.

The difference between
G_{6,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
and
G_{7,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
is whether
\widetilde{k_{b}}
is
generated by
\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}}
or uniformly random.
Since
C
distinguishes between the honestly generated key and a uniformly random key,
replacing this key with the key
k_{i}
for which we have the same question
allows distinguishing an honestly generated key from a uniformly random key for
the
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}
game.
Since this is true for all adversaries
C
, we have

\left|\Pr[G_{6,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{7,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{2})\kern 5.0pt.

(10)

Games
G_{8,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}

Here, we replace the output of the
KDF
of the test session with a uniformly random
value. The value is generated at
Item

11
and used at
Items

44
and
70
.

Since we model
\textsf{KDF}:\{0,1\}^{2\ell_{\texttt{stat}}+\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}}
as a random oracle, we can use one-way to hiding (
Lemma

1
)
to bound the distance between
G_{7,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
and
G_{8,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
.
Define
H(\cdot)=\textsf{KDF}(k_{a},\cdot,k_{e})
where
k_{a}
and
k_{e}
are the
keys used in session
s_{\mathit{init}}
.
Then for any fixed
k_{a}
,
k_{e}
we can replace sampling

\textsf{KDF}\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{2\ell_{\texttt{stat}}+\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})
with sampling

H\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})
.
Let
B^{H}(x,y)
be the execution of
G_{7,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}

where we replace
k_{b}
of the test session with
x
,
and
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
of the test session with
y
.
Then

\Pr[G_{7,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[b^{\prime}=1:b^{\prime}\leftarrow B^{H}(x,H(x))]

and

\Pr[G_{8,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[b^{\prime}=1:y\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}},b^{\prime}\leftarrow B^{H}(x,y)]

where we also take the probability over
H\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})

and
x\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}
.
As in
Lemma

1
, let
C
be an oracle algorithm that on input
x
does the following: pick

i\leftarrow_{\$}\{1,...,q_{h}\}
and
y\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{m}
, run
B^{H}(x,y)
until just before
the
i
-th query, measure the argument of the query in the computational basis,
and output the measurement outcome.
We define

P_{C}=\Pr[x=x^{\prime}:x^{\prime}\leftarrow C^{H}(x)]

where we also take the probability over
H\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})
and
x\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}
.
But since
x
is not available to
C
at any point, the best the attacker can do is guess,
which means that
P_{C}=\frac{1}{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}
.

Then by
Lemma

1
we have

\left|\Pr[G_{7,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{8,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq 2q_{H}\sqrt{P_{C}}=2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}.

(11)

Next, we want to bound

\left|\Pr[G_{8,0}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{8,1}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|.

Key
k_{0}^{*}
is generated as a part
k_{\mathit{pqc}}\oplus k_{\mathit{qkd}}
, where
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
is uniformly random.
Furthermore, this key is only used in the matching session of the test session, which
the adversary is not allowed to reveal.
Key
k_{1}^{*}
is generated uniformly random. Therefore the adversary cannot distinguish between

G_{8,0}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
and
G_{8,1}^{{\neg sk(i)}}
, and we have

\Pr[G_{8,0}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{8,1}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1].

(12)

Combining equations (
9
), (
10
) and (
11
), we get that

\left|\Pr[G_{5,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{8,b}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{2})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}.

Combining this with (
12
), we get that

\left|\Pr[G_{5,0}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{5,1}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq 2\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+2\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{2})+4q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}

which, when combined with equations (
7
) and (
8
), gives

\left|\Pr[G_{3,0}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\wedge\neg sk(i)\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{3,1}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\wedge\neg sk(i)\Rightarrow 1]\right|\\
\leq 2\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\left(\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{2})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}\right)

(13)

Combining
Section

5.1
and
Section

5.1

Combining
Eqs.

6
and
13
gives

\left|\Pr[G_{3,0}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{3,1}^{{\neg sk(i)}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\\
\leq 2\delta_{\mathit{eph}}+2\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CPA}}(B_{1})+4q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}}}+2\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\left(\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{2})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

If we fill this in
Eq.

1
, we have proven the claim of
Lemma

2

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}\neq\emptyset]\right|

\leq\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\cdot\left|\Pr[G_{3,1}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{3,0}\Rightarrow 1]\right|+2\cdot\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})

\leq 2\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\!^{2}\cdot\left(\delta_{\mathit{eph}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{eph}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CPA}}(B_{1})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}}}+\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\left(\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{2})+2q_{H}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}\right)\right)

+2\cdot\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\cdot\mu(\textsf{Encaps}_{\mathit{stat}})

5.2
Security proof for
Section

5.1
: active attack,
Lemma

3

We consider the case that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}=\emptyset
,
so we are proving an upper bound for:

|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}=\emptyset]\\
-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}=\emptyset]|.

Proof

We consider the initiator and responder session separately,
but due to the symmetry in the protocol the proofs are very similar.
We consider only adversaries for which
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textsf{AttackPQC}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}})
does not hold.
We know
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}=\emptyset
by assumption of the case,
so that by definition of
AttackPQC
, we have

\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textsf{AttackPQC}}(\emptyset)\Leftrightarrow\\
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revState}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sesKey}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]\neq\bot\land\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{revealed}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]\land\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{corrupted}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]].

In other words, the encapsulation to the peer’s static key
leaks neither directly via a state reveal,
nor indirectly via corruption of the peer.
We prove this by reduction to the
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CCA}
security of
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}
.
There may be a partially matching session that computes the same
k_{\mathit{pqc}}

(we consider that in
Proof
).
Since we are considering active attacks,
the full transcript must differ somewhere between these sessions,
so (unless a forgery against
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
was made),
the initiator will detect this difference and abort.
If the test session is a responder,
then this means that its value
k_{\mathit{pqc},s}
is independent of the adversary view
and therefore the session key is indistinguishable from uniformly random.
If there does not exist a partially matching session
(we consider this in
Proof
),
then
k_{\mathit{pqc},s}
looks random for the adversary
and therefore so does the session key.

Game
