The game hops are given in
Fig.

17
.
First we formalize the assumption of
Section

5.1
:
we encode
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}=\emptyset

in
G_{1,b}^{\neg\text{ms}}
, so that

\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{StAA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{PQC}_{b}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{matches}}=\emptyset]=\Pr[G_{1,b}^{\neg\text{ms}}\Rightarrow 1].

(14)

Next,
G_{2,b}^{\neg\text{ms}}
makes a random guess
s^{\prime}
for the test session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
,
and since
\Pr[s^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]=1/\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}
and we return 0 if the guess was wrong:

\Pr[G_{1,b}^{\neg\text{ms}}\Rightarrow 1]=\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\Pr[G_{2,b}^{\neg\text{ms}}\Rightarrow 1].

(15)

G_{3,b}^{\neg\text{ms}}
makes a random guess
j^{\prime}
for the test session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]
,
and since
\Pr[j^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]]=1/\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}
:

\Pr[G_{2,b}^{\neg\text{ms}}\Rightarrow 1]=\mathit{n_{\texttt{p\!\!\>t}}}\Pr[G_{3,b}^{\neg\text{ms}}\Rightarrow 1].

(16)

Proof
: Test session is an initiator.
We first consider the case that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Initiator}}}
,
encoded in
G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
:

\Pr[G_{3,b}^{\neg\text{ms}}\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Initiator}}}]=\Pr[G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1].

(17)

In game
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
, we sample the test-session encapsulation in advance,
and we also replace the decapsulated value with the encapsulated one.
The games are identical unless a decapsulation failure occurs in
G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
,
which by
Definition

1
happens with probability at most
\delta_{\mathit{stat}}
, so we have

|\Pr[G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1]|\leq\delta_{\mathit{stat}}.

(18)

Game
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
samples a uniformly random
\widetilde{k_{b}}

and overwrites the encapsulated value.
Given an adversary
D
that distinguishes

G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
from
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}

with advantage
\mathrm{Adv}(D)
,
we define an adversary
B_{3}
that uses
D

and wins the
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CCA}
game of
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}
with
\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{3})=\mathrm{Adv}(D)
.

Adversary
B_{3}
gets input
(\hat{\mathit{pk}},\hat{c},\hat{k})

and can query the decapsulation oracle
\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}{\textsc{Dec}}(\hat{\mathit{sk}},\cdot)

on all inputs except
\hat{c}
.

B_{3}
wins if they can correctly say whether
\hat{c}
is an encapsulation of
\hat{k}

or if
\hat{k}
is a uniformly random value.

B_{3}
runs
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}

except that they replace the public key of the guessed session
\mathit{pk}_{j^{\prime}}
with
\hat{\mathit{pk}}

in
Items

12
and
36
,
they replace
(\widetilde{c_{b}},\widetilde{k_{b}})
with
(\hat{c},\hat{k})
on
Item

09
,
and instead of decapsulating with
\mathit{sk}_{j^{\prime}}
they query the oracle in
Item

50

(making sure to skip the query on input
\hat{c}
and directly output
\hat{k}
instead).
Finally,
B_{3}
outputs whatever
D
outputs.

If
B_{3}
receives a real encapsulation they are running
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
,
otherwise
\hat{k}
was random and they are running
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
.
We also see that
B_{3}
is an
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CCA}
adversary,
so that

|\Pr[G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1]|\leq\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{3}).

(19)

To prepare for the next case distinction,
in
G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
we record the input to the
KDF
in the
kdfIn
associative array.
Since this bookkeeping does not change the adversary view, we have

\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1].

(20)

Proof
. A KDF input match exists.

In this case, we assume that there exists an honest session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}]
.
We formalize this condition in
G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
on
Item

25
.

\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1\land\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}]]=\Pr[G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(21)

Game
G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
makes a guess
s^{\prime\prime}
for the session such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[s^{\prime\prime}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]
,
and we return 0 if our guess turned out to be wrong.
Since
G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
established that such a session must exist
and our guess is uniformly random over all sessions other than
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
, we have that

\Pr[G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]=(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-1)\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]

(22)

Game
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
establishes that
s^{\prime\prime}
is unique.
Game
G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
and
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
are identical
unless
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
returns 0 on
Item

30
.
A collision in
kdfIn
requires at least a collision in
k_{a}
,
but by
Definition

2
we know that such a collision occurs with
probability at most
\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})
.
There are at most
\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-2
sessions that can collide,
so (also by the difference lemma) we get

|\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]|\\
\leq\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\text{ returns on \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{line:kdfUnique}}]\leq(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-2)\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})\leq\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}}).

(23)

Game
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
replaces
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
with the same uniformly random sampled value
in both
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
and
s^{\prime\prime}
.
Since we model
\textsf{KDF}:\{0,1\}^{2\ell_{\texttt{stat}}+\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}}
as a random oracle, we can use the one-way to hiding
Lemma

1
.
Similarly to the hop from
G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
to
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{st}}
, we
define
H(\cdot)=\textsf{KDF}(k_{a},\cdot,k_{e})
where
k_{a}
and

k_{e}
are the keys used in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[s^{\prime\prime}]
.

Then for any fixed
k_{a}
,
k_{e}
we can replace sampling

\textsf{KDF}\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{2\ell_{\texttt{stat}}+\ell_{\texttt{eph}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})
with sampling

H\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})
.
Let
B^{H}(x,y)
be the execution of
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}

where we replace
\widetilde{k_{b}}
of the test session with
x
,
and
\widetilde{k_{\mathit{pqc}}}
of the test session with
y
.
Then

\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[b^{\prime}=1:b^{\prime}\leftarrow B^{H}(x,H(x))]

and

\Pr[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[b^{\prime}=1:b^{\prime}\leftarrow B^{H^{\prime}}(x,y),y\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}}]

where we also take the average over
H\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})
and
x\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}
.
As in
Lemma

1
, let
C
be an oracle algorithm that on input
x
does the following: pick

i\leftarrow_{\$}\{1,...,q_{h}\}
and
y\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}}
, run
B^{H}(x,y)
until just before
the
i
-th query, measure the argument of the query in the computational basis,
and output the measurement outcome.

We define

P_{C}=\Pr[x=x^{\prime}:x^{\prime}\leftarrow C^{H}(x)]

where the probability is also over
H\leftarrow_{\$}(\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{pqc}}})
and
x\leftarrow_{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}
.
But since
x
is not available to
C
at any point, the best the attacker can do is guess,
which means that
P_{C}=\frac{1}{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}
.

Then by
Lemma

1
we have

\left|\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq 2q_{H}\sqrt{P_{C}}=\frac{2q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}.

(24)

Game
G_{12,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
always aborts the test session.
To prove this is indistinguishable from
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
we consider two cases,
based on whether the transcript matches on all values before the second MAC tag.
Specifically,
let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sent}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]=m_{1}^{*}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{received}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]=m_{2}^{*}=(c_{a}^{*},c_{e}^{*},\mathit{kID}^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},\tau_{2}^{*})

be the transcript of the test session,
then we denote
t^{*}=(m_{1}^{*},(c_{a}^{*},c_{e}^{*},\mathit{kID}^{*}))
.
Similarly we write
(t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},\tau_{2}^{\prime\prime})
for the values recorded in

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sent}}}[s^{\prime\prime}]
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{received}}}[s^{\prime\prime}]
.

Proof
.

In this case the session
s^{\prime\prime}

almost
matches the test session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
:
the transcript matches up to and including the first tag,
and both sessions have indicated each other as their peers.
Specifically in this case
(t^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}])=(t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[s^{\prime\prime}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[s^{\prime\prime}])
.
The tag
\tau_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\allowbreak\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}_{k_{\mathit{pqc},m}}((t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},\allowbreak{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[s^{\prime\prime}],\allowbreak{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[s^{\prime\prime}]))
was generated in session
s^{\prime\prime}
.
Since
s^{\prime\prime}
is not a matching session of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
,
it must hold that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
received a different tag:
\tau_{2}^{*}\neq\tau_{2}^{\prime\prime}
.
But since
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
(and therefore
k_{\mathit{pqc},m}
) has the same value in both
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
and
s^{\prime\prime}
,
both the key and message of the
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
are identical
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
will recompute
\tau_{2}^{\prime\prime}
during verification, and therefore reject
\tau_{2}^{*}
.
In this case,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
will therefore abort with certainty:

\Pr[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1\land(t^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}])=(t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[s^{\prime\prime}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[s^{\prime\prime}])]\\
=\Pr[G_{12,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(25)

Proof
.

In this case the session
s^{\prime\prime}
differs from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
somewhere in the partial transcript,
or the sessions do not indicate each other as peers.
This means the input to
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}_{k_{\mathit{pqc},m}}
will be different,
from which we can give a reduction to the
\mathsf{OT}
-
\mathsf{sEUF}
-
\mathsf{CMA}
security of
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
.
Specifically in this case
(t^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}])\neq(t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[s^{\prime\prime}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[s^{\prime\prime}])
.
Note that
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
and
G_{12,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
are identical,
unless the failure event occurs
where
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
does
not
abort session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
on
Item

94
,
so we bound the probability of this failure event.
Let
D
be the adversary that triggers the failure event.
We construct the following adversary against the
\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}^{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)},B_{4}}
game.

B_{4}=(B_{4,0},B_{4,1})
runs
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
using
D
as a subroutine, but with the following modifications:
in session
s^{\prime\prime}
on
Item

65
, instead of computing
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
,

B_{4,0}
outputs
m=(t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[s^{\prime\prime}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[s^{\prime\prime}])
and gets back a tag
\tau
from the
\mathsf{OT}
-
\mathsf{sEUF}
-
\mathsf{CMA}
game:

B_{4}
sets
\tau_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\tau
in
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
.
Then on
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\textsc{SEND-M2}}(s^{\prime},m_{2})
,

B_{4,1}
outputs
(m^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})=((t^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]),\tau_{2}^{*})
.
Note that both
k_{\mathit{pqc},m}
in
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
and
k
in the
\mathsf{OT}
-
\mathsf{sEUF}
-
\mathsf{CMA}
game are uniformly random keys,
that
m\neq m^{\prime}
by the assumption of
Proof
,
and that
B_{4}
created a valid forgery exactly when
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
would not abort, so that

\Pr\big[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\text{ does not abort on \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{line:verifytags}}\land\\
(t^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}])\neq(t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[s^{\prime\prime}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[s^{\prime\prime}])\big]=\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{4})

and thus by the difference lemma

\Big|\Pr\left[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1\land(t^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}])\neq(t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[s^{\prime\prime}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[s^{\prime\prime}])\right]\\
-\Pr[G_{12,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]\Big|\leq\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{4}).

(26)

We combine disjoint
Proof
and
Proof
by taking the sum over
Eqs.

25
and
26
,
and we get

|\Pr[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{12,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]|\leq\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{4}).

(27)

Since both
G_{12,0}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
and

always abort the test session,

,

and

concludes
Proof
, we get

\big|\Pr[G_{7,0}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1\land\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}]]\\
-\Pr[G_{7,1}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1\land\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}]]\big|\\
\leq 2(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-1)\left(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})+\frac{2q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{4})\right)

Figure 18:
Game
G_{\cdot,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
: non-matching sessions, initiator session, matching
kdfIn
does not exist

If no KDF input match exists,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]

for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
.
We encode this in
G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}
in
Fig.

18
, so that

\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1\land\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]]=\Pr[G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(30)

Game
G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}
replaces
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
with a random value
\widetilde{k_{\mathit{pqc}}}
.
Since this step replaces a single QROM output,
identical to the gamehop from
G_{12,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
to
G_{13,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
,
we have

|\Pr[G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]|=|\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]|\\
\leq 2q_{H}\sqrt{P_{C}}=\frac{2q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}.

(31)

From here we could prove that the initiator will abort,
unless the adversary creates a forgery against
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
.
However, even a forgery will not help the adversary,
because the key that would be accepted is uniformly random,
which we prove next.

Game
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}
replaces
k_{\mathit{sess}}
with a random value.
Since
k_{\mathit{pqc},s}
acts as a one-time pad on
k_{\mathit{qkd},s}
,
this replacement does not change the view of the adversary, so that

\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(32)

Both
k^{*}_{0}
and
k^{*}_{1}
are uniformly random keys in
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}
,
so that

\Pr[G_{10,0}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{10,1}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(33)

So that for
Proof
, we combine
Eqs.

30
,
31
,
32
and
33
to get

|\Pr[G_{7,0}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1\land\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]]\\
-\Pr[G_{7,1}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1\land\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]]|\leq\frac{4q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}.

(34)

To conclude mutually exclusive
Proof
and
Proof
, we take the sum over
Eqs.

29
and
34
and get

|\Pr[G_{7,0}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{7,1}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1]|\\
\leq 2(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-1)\left(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})+\frac{2q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{4})\right)+\frac{4q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}\\
\leq 2\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\left(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})+\frac{2q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{4})\right).

(35)

so that for
Proof
we combine
Eqs.

17
,
18
,
19
,
20
and
35
to conclude

|\Pr[G_{3,0}^{\neg\text{ms}}\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Initiator}}}]-\Pr[G_{3,1}^{\neg\text{ms}}\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Initiator}}}]|\\
\leq 2\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+2\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{3})+2\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\left(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})+\frac{q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}+\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{4})\right)\,.

(36)

Proof

Figure 19:
Game
G_{\cdot,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}
: non-matching sessions, responder test session

Formally,
Proof
occurs when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Responder}}}
,
this is encoded in
G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}
(see
Fig.

19
):

\Pr[G_{3,b}^{\neg\text{ms}}\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{role}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Responder}}}]=\Pr[G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1].

(37)

In game
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}
, we sample the test-session static encapsulation in advance,
and in initiator sessions we replace the decapsulated value with the encapsulated one.
The games are identical unless a decapsulation failure occurs in
G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}
,
which by
Definition

1
is at most
\delta_{\mathit{stat}}
, so we have

|\Pr[G_{4,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1]|\leq\delta_{\mathit{stat}}.

(38)

Game
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}
samples a uniformly random
\widetilde{k_{a}}

and overwrites the encapsulated value.
Given an adversary
D
that distinguishes

G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}
from
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}

with advantage
\mathrm{Adv}(D)
,
we define an adversary
B_{5}
that uses
D

and wins the
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CCA}
game of
\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}
with
\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{5})=\mathrm{Adv}(D)
.

Adversary
B
gets input
(\hat{\mathit{pk}},\hat{c},\hat{k})

and can query the decapsulation oracle
\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}{\textsc{Dec}}(\hat{\mathit{sk}},\cdot)

on all inputs except
\hat{c}
.

B_{5}
wins if they can correctly say whether
\hat{c}
is an encapsulation of
\hat{k}

or if
\hat{k}
is a uniformly random value.

B_{5}
runs
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}

except that they replace the public key of the guessed session
\mathit{pk}_{j^{\prime}}
with
\hat{\mathit{pk}}

in
Items

13
and
40
,
they replace
(\widetilde{c_{a}},\widetilde{k_{a}})
with
(\hat{c},\hat{k})
on
Item

09
,
and instead of decapsulating with
\mathit{sk}_{j^{\prime}}
they query the oracle in
Item

69

(making sure to skip the query on input
\hat{c}
and directly output
\hat{k}
instead).
Finally,
B_{5}
outputs whatever
D
outputs.

If
B_{5}
receives a real encapsulation they are running
G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
,
otherwise
\hat{k}
was random and they are running
G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}
.
We also see that
B_{5}
is an
\mathsf{IND}
-
\mathsf{CCA}
adversary,
so that

|\Pr[G_{5,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1]|\leq\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{KEM}_{\mathit{stat}}}^{\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CCA}}(B_{5}).

(39)

To prepare for the next case distinction,

G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}
records the input to the
KDF
in the
kdfIn
associative array.
Since this bookkeeping does not change the adversary view, we have

\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1].

(40)

Proof
. A KDF input match exists.

In this case, we assume that there exists an honest session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}]
.
We formalize this condition in
G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}
:

\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1\land\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime}]]=\Pr[G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(41)

Game
G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
makes a guess
s^{\prime\prime}
for the session such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[s^{\prime\prime}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]
,
and we return 0 if our guess turned out to be wrong.
Since
G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
established that such a session must exist
and our guess is uniformly random over all sessions other than
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
, we have that

\Pr[G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]=(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-1)\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(42)

Game
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
establishes that
s^{\prime\prime}
is unique.
Game
G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
and
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
are identical
unless
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
returns on
Item

31
.
A collision in
kdfIn
requires at least a collision in
k_{b}
,
but by
Definition

2
, we know that such a collision occurs with
probability at most
\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})
.
There are at most
\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-2
sessions that can collide,
so (also by the difference lemma) we get

|\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]|\leq\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\text{ returns on \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{line:nmrkKdfUnique}}]\\
\leq(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-2)\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}})\leq\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\mu(\textsf{Secret}_{\mathit{stat}}).

(43)

Game
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
replaces
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
with the same uniformly random sampled value
in both
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
and

.
We are replacing an output of the QROM,
which is analogous to the gamehop from
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}
to
G_{12,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}

so we have

\left|\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|=\left|\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\\
\leq 2q_{H}\sqrt{P_{C}}=\frac{2q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}.

(44)

Game
G_{12,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
always aborts initiator session
s^{\prime\prime}
.
To prove this is indistinguishable from
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
we consider two cases,
based on whether the transcript matches on all values before the second MAC tag.
Specifically,
let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{received}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]=m_{1}^{*}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sent}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]=m_{2}^{*}=(c_{a}^{*},c_{e}^{*},\mathit{kID}^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},\tau_{2}^{*})

be the transcript of the test session,
then we denote
t^{*}=(m_{1}^{*},(c_{a}^{*},c_{e}^{*},\mathit{kID}^{*}))
.
Similarly we write
(t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},\tau_{2}^{\prime\prime})
for the values recorded in

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{sent}}}[s^{\prime\prime}]
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{received}}}[s^{\prime\prime}]
.

Proof
.

In this case the session
s^{\prime\prime}

almost
matches the test session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
:
the transcript matches up to and including the first tag,
and both sessions have indicated each other as their peers.
Specifically in this case
(t^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}])=(t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[s^{\prime\prime}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[s^{\prime\prime}])
.
The tag
\tau_{2}^{*}=\allowbreak\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}_{k_{\mathit{pqc},m}}((t^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]))

was generated in session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
.
Since
s^{\prime\prime}
is not a matching session of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
,
it must hold that
s^{\prime\prime}
received a different tag:
\tau_{2}^{\prime\prime}\neq\tau_{2}^{*}
.
But since
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
(and therefore
k_{\mathit{pqc},m}
) has the same value in both
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
and
s^{\prime\prime}
,
both the key and message of the
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
are identical
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{\prime\prime}
will recompute
\tau_{2}^{*}
during verification, and therefore reject
\tau_{2}^{\prime\prime}
.
In this case,
s^{\prime\prime}
will therefore abort with certainty:

\Pr[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1\land(t^{*},\tau_{1}^{*},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}])=(t^{\prime\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[s^{\prime\prime}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[s^{\prime\prime}])]\\
=\Pr[G_{12,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(45)

Proof
.

In this case the session

Figure 20:
Game
G_{\cdot,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}
: non-matching sessions, responder test session, no matching
kdfIn

If no KDF input match exists,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]

for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
.
We encode this in
G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}
in
Fig.

20
, so that

\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1\land\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]]=\Pr[G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(51)

Game
G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}
replaces
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
with a random value
\widetilde{k_{\mathit{pqc}}}
.
Since this step replaces a single QROM output,
identical to the gamehop from
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
to
G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}
,
we have

|\Pr[G_{8,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]|=|\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{11,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]|\\
\leq 2q_{H}\sqrt{P_{C}}=\frac{2q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}.

(52)

Game
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}
replaces
k_{\mathit{sess}}
with a random value.
Since
k_{\mathit{pqc},s}
acts as a one-time pad on
k_{\mathit{qkd},s}
,
this does not change the view of the adversary, so that

\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(53)

Both
k^{*}_{0}
and
k^{*}_{1}
are uniformly random keys in
G_{10,b}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}
,
so that

\Pr[G_{10,0}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{10,1}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{r},\neg\text{k}}\Rightarrow 1].

(54)

So that combining
Eqs.

51
,
52
,
53
and
54
for

-\Pr[G_{7,1}^{\neg\text{ms},\text{i}}\Rightarrow 1\land\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kdfIn}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}]]|\leq\frac{4q_{H}}{\sqrt{2^{\ell_{\texttt{stat}}}}}.

5.3
Security proof for QKD-based security,
Theorem

5.3

Proof
(QKD-based security)

We consider security for the
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}
game, as defined in
Fig.

8
,
so that we are proving an upper bound for

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}_{0}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}_{1}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]\right|.

By the assumption that QKD is not (trivially) broken,
the QKD key used by the test session is
Honest
,
so it is uniformly random and has not leaked to the adversary directly via an
QKD-LEAK
or an
QKD-OVERRIDE
query.
The QKD key may have been delivered to one other session,
so the main hurdle for proving
Theorem

5.3
is to prove that the other session did not leak the QKD key.
Since the QKD key is not part of the state and
k_{\mathit{qkd},s}
is independent from other values,
this could only occur through a
REVEAL
query.
If the other session is a completed matching session,
then
REVEAL
would constitute a trivial attack.
Otherwise the transcript of the other session differs from that of the test session,
in that case the
Initiator
will reject (unless a
\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}
was forged).

Figure 21:

Game
G^{\text{qkd}}
: QKD is secure.

Game
G_{0}^{\text{qkd}}
equals
\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}_{b}
:

\Pr[\mathsf{IND}{}\text{-}\mathsf{AA}{}\text{-}\mathsf{QKD}_{b}(\Pi,A)\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{0,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1].

(59)

Game
G_{1,b}^{\text{qkd}}
adds
Items

50
and
81
,
tracking per session which QKD key IDs they use in the associative array
kidUsed
,
which are initialized at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{kidUsed}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}]=[\ ]
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}
.
Since this is only doing some bookkeeping, we have

\Pr[G_{0,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{1,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1].

(60)

Game
G_{2,b}^{\text{qkd}}
asserts that the test session used just a single
\mathit{kID}
,
and labels it
\mathit{kID}^{*}
.
The Send queries of accepting sessions
must be queried exactly once and in order:

SEND-INIT
and
SEND-M2
for an initiator
and
SEND-M1
for a responder.
This is enforced by the
state
variable,
where we note that for an initiator this enforcement is implicit
since
Item

73
of
SendM2

aborts if the
state
does not hold a triple.
In the protocol a session queries the QKD oracle only once
(a responder in
Item

49
and an initiator in
Item

79
),
so that each session uses
at most
one QKD key,
and each accepting session uses
exactly
one QKD key.
Thus the check on
Item

14
will always be false, so that

\Pr[G_{0,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{1,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1].

(61)

Next we consider the other session that shares the QKD key with the test session,
if it exists.
First, define
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qSentSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]
.
Note that this session always exists (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}\neq\bot
):
only an honest query to
QKD-GET-KEY
can set
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qStatus}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]
to
Honest
,
and this also sets
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qSentSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]
.
Second, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]\neq[\ ]
, then define
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
such that
[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]
.
Note that
|{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]|\leq 1
even if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*}
,
because
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]
is only updated by
QKD-GET-KEY-WITH-ID
({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}})
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qKey}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]\neq\bot
,
and an
Honest
key will be deleted after the first call to
QKD-GET-KEY-WITH-ID
({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}})
.
This proves that there is at most one completed matching session.

Each responder session calls
QKD-GET-KEY
once and increments
\mathit{kID}_{\mathit{ctr}}
,
so there is only one honest responder session that contains
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}
in
sent
.
Since the QKD key is
Honest
, it is only delivered to one initiator session.
Therefore there is at most one honest initiator session that
received a key from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textsc{QKD-GET-KEY-WITH-ID}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}})
,
implying there is at most one accepting initiator session with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}
in
received
.

Game
G_{3,b}^{\text{qkd}}
makes a guess
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}^{\prime}
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
and returns 0 if the guess was incorrect.

Item

17
checks this by comparing the guess
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}^{\prime}
against
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qSentSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]
,
which equals
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
by definition.
Since
\Pr[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}]=1/\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}
, we have

\Pr[G_{2,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1]=\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}\Pr[G_{3,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1].

(62)

Game
G_{4,b}^{\text{qkd}}
samples
\widetilde{k_{\mathit{qkd}}}

and replaces
k_{\mathit{qkd}}
as sampled by the QKD oracle
with
\widetilde{k_{\mathit{qkd}}}
in sessions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
and (if it exists) in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
.
The predicate on
Item

82
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qSentSid}}}[\mathit{kID}]
)
is equivalent to the predicate
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
,
except it will be false for all sessions if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
does not exist.
The equivalence holds since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qStatus}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\text{{Honest}}}
,
and thus the QKD key is delivered to at most one initiator session.
For any other adversary queries
SEND-M2
containing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}
,

QKD-GET-KEY-WITH-ID
will return
\bot
and those sessions will abort on
Item

80
.
Since all we did was move the uniform sampling from the oracle to the game, we have

\Pr[G_{3,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{4,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1].

(63)

Game
G_{9,b}^{\text{qkd}}
replaces
k_{\mathit{sess}}
with the uniformly random sampled
\widetilde{k_{\mathit{sess}}}
.
Note that
k_{\mathit{qkd},s}
acts as a one-time pad on
k_{\mathit{pqc},s}
,
meaning that this gamehop is indistinguishable if
k_{\mathit{qkd},s}^{*}

(the value of
k_{\mathit{qkd},s}
used by the test session)
is independent of the adversary view (without the test session key).
We will prove this by case distinction.

Proof
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
does not exist.

In this case we assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]=[\ ]
:
no honest session received the test session QKD key.
Then
k_{\mathit{qkd},s}^{*}
only exists in session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}

(and it must hold that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}^{*})
, so that

\Pr\left[G_{4,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]=[\ ]\right]=\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1].

(64)

Proof
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
exists.

In this case we assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]=[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}]
. This is encoded in game
G_{5,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r}}
, so that

\Pr\left[G_{4,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qRecvSid}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}]=[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}]\right]=\Pr[G_{5,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1].

(65)

We prove
G_{5,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r}}
is indistinguishable from
G_{9,b}^{\text{qkd}}
by looking at several subcases,
based on whether or not the transcripts and/or tags are equal.
Let
t_{\mathit{qRecv}}=((c_{b},\mathit{pk}_{e}),(c_{a}^{\prime},c_{e}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}))
be the partial transcript of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
,
and
t_{\mathit{qSent}}=((c_{b}^{\prime},\mathit{pk}_{e}^{\prime}),(c_{a},c_{e},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{kID}^{*}}))
be the partial transcript of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
.
Similarly let
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
be the tags sent by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
,
and let
\tau_{1}^{\prime},\tau_{2}^{\prime}
be the tags as received by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
.

Proof
: transcripts differ.

This case assumes
t_{\mathit{qRecv}}\neq t_{\mathit{qSent}}
. This is encoded in game
G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}
,
so that

\Pr[G_{5,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1\land t_{\mathit{qRecv}}\neq t_{\mathit{qSent}}]=\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1].

(66)

Game
G_{7,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}
always aborts session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
.
Note that
G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}
and
G_{7,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}
are identical unless

G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}
does not abort session
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
on
Item

98
.
Then by the difference lemma, we have

\left|\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}\text{ does not abort }{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}\text{ on \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{line:c3-tauabort}}],

This event would require at least
\tau_{1}=\tau_{1}^{\prime}
to hold in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
,
which lets us bound this by a reduction to the
\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}
security of
\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}
,
using the following adversary
B_{7}=(B_{7,0},B_{7,1})
:

B_{7}
runs game
G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}
, with the following modifications.
On
Item

61
, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}^{\prime}
,
then instead of computing
\tau
directly,
B_{7,0}
outputs

m=(t_{\mathit{qSent}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}])

and uses the response
\tau
.
Just before
Item

98
,
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{qSentSid}}}[\mathit{kID}]
(ie. if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
),

B_{7,1}
outputs

(m^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})=((t_{\mathit{qRecv}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}]),\tau_{1}^{\prime})
.
Then
m\neq m^{\prime}
, and
B_{7}
created a successful forgery exactly when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
accepts
\tau_{1}^{\prime}
.
Thus

\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}\text{ does not abort }{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}\text{ on \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{line:c3-tauabort}}]\leq\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{7})

and therefore

\left|\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1]-\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1]\right|\leq\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{7}).

(67)

Since
G_{7,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}
always aborts
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
,
k_{\mathit{qkd},s}^{*}
only exists in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
, so that:

\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\neg\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1]=\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1].

(68)

We combine
Eqs.

66
,
67
and
68
to conclude that for
Proof
:

|\Pr[G_{5,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1\land t_{\mathit{qRecv}}\neq t_{\mathit{qSent}}]-\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1]|\leq\mathrm{Adv}_{\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}}^{\mathsf{OT}{}\text{-}\mathsf{sEUF}{}\text{-}\mathsf{CMA}}(B_{7}).

(69)

Proof
: transcripts are the same.

In this case:
t_{\mathit{qRecv}}=t_{\mathit{qSent}}
. Game
G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}
encodes this:

\Pr[G_{5,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r}}\Rightarrow 1\land t_{\mathit{qRecv}}=t_{\mathit{qSent}}]=\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1].

(70)

By definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
, both sessions use the same
k_{\mathit{qkd}}
,
and thus the same
k_{\mathit{qkd},m}
.
Note that
({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}],\allowbreak{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}])=({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}])
,
because
QKD-GET-KEY-WITH-ID
checked this.
We conclude that both sessions have the same key and message input to
\textsf{MAC}^{(qkd)}
,
so they compute the same tag
\tau_{1}
.
If the adversary changed
\tau_{1}^{\prime}\neq\tau_{1}
, then the initiator will abort, therefore:

\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1\land\tau_{1}^{\prime}\neq\tau_{1}]=\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1].

(71)

Next we prove that if
t_{\mathit{qRecv}}=t_{\mathit{qSent}}
,
then almost certainly (by correctness of the
KEM
s)
both sessions compute the same
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
.
Game
G_{7,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}
makes a uniformly random guess
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}^{\prime}

for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
and aborts if the guess was incorrect.
Since the guess is correct with probability
1/(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-1)
, we have

\Pr[G_{6,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1\land\tau_{1}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}]=(\mathit{n_{\texttt{s}}}-1)\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1\land\tau_{1}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}].

(72)

Game
G_{8,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}
records the encapsulated secrets in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}

and replaces the decapsulation outputs in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
with the recorded values,
and vice versa. By the correctness of the two static
KEM
s and the one
ephemeral
KEM
, we have

|\Pr[G_{7,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1\land\tau_{1}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}]-\Pr[G_{8,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1\land\tau_{1}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}]|\leq 2\delta_{\mathit{stat}}+\delta_{\mathit{eph}}.

(73)

We emphasize that the
KEM
correctness is a statistical property and not a computational one,
since the probability in
Definition

1
is independent of any adversary.

In
G_{8,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}
, both
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
compute the same
k_{\mathit{pqc}}
,
and thus the same
k_{\mathit{pqc},m}
.
Since
t_{\mathit{qRecv}}=t_{\mathit{qSent}}
,
\tau_{1}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}
, and

({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}])=({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{owner}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\textup{{peer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}])
,
both key and message input to
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
are the same in both sessions.
That means that if the adversary sends
\tau_{2}^{\prime}\neq\tau_{2}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}
will reject with certainty since
\textsf{MAC}^{(pqc)}
is assumed to be canonical.

Statistical strong unforgeability would suffice but induces as small loss,

On the other hand, if
\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\tau_{2}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qSent}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathit{sID}}_{\mathit{qRecv}}

and the adversary may not query

on either session.
In either case,

\Pr[G_{8,b}^{\text{qkd},\text{r},\text{mt}}\Rightarrow 1\land\tau_{1}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}]=\Pr[G_{9,b}^{\text{qkd}}\Rightarrow 1].

(74)

We combine

(75)

summing

(76)

6
Conclusion and Future Work

In this paper we identify some gaps within state-of-the-art modelling of combined
QKD and PQC authenticated key exchange,
most notably we consider the omission of QKD key IDs in the models.
We demonstrate that this omission allows an adversary to mix-and-match QKD keys of different sessions,
which could lead to full leakage of the session keys.
To enable the proper handling of key IDs in cryptographic contexts, we propose to model QKD as an oracle
that explicitly models key IDs as input and output of oracle queries.
The oracle provides queries for honest parties to get QKD keys,
and it provides adversarial queries to model both passive and active attacks on QKD.

To demonstrate the usefulness of the QKD oracle,
we construct a QKD/PQC-combined protocol that generates secure session keys
if either QKD is secure or the PQC (or PQC/classical-combined) primitives are.
We emphasize that our protocol preserves the information-theoretic security provided by QKD.
Our solution uses nested
MAC
s to prevent Dependent-Key attacks,
a construction that is inspired by the XOR-then-MAC
KEM
-combiner,
but which avoids some of the pitfalls we identified in its security proof.

Future work.

Our QKD oracle is modular and can be integrated models other than CK
+
:
many variations on AKE models exist and (in principle) the oracle could
be integrated into any of them,
allowing formal modelling of other QKD/PQC-combined protocols.
For example, it could be integrated into HAKE,
so that the security for the Muckle family of protocols could be proven
when QKD key IDs are included into the protocol.

One noteworthy advantage of the HAKE model over ours is that it captures Post-Compromise Security (PCS)
[
CCG16
]
,
which allows some security even after session compromise.
PCS seems a meaningful property to have in some contexts,
we view it as an interesting future direction to combine our QKD oracle
with AKE models that model PCS (such as HAKE).

We identified an open problem in
Section

3.1
about the construction
of robust one-time strong unforgeable
MAC
s for the XtM
KEM
combiner.
Future work could explore the existence of such a
MAC
,
or it might be possible to fix the proof for the XtM combiner.

Our QKD oracle returns uniformly random bitstrings,
while real QKD can only guarantee statistical security:
the output key is statistically close to uniformly random.
The oracle could be adapted into a more accurate representation of reality
if this QKD security parameter is reflected in its output.

The QKD oracle presented in this paper is designed to model
end-to-end QKD
.
Many deployments of QKD instead secure point-to-point links in a larger network,
and trusted repeater nodes are used to relay messages and/or keys.
While the QKD oracle could be used to output relayed keys,
we point out that this is a strong assumption on the security of both QKD and on the relaying protocol itself.
Instead for relaying protocols,
it would be more realistic to use the QKD oracle only to model point-to-point links,
and to precisely state the trust assumptions that are required
in order to guarantee security of the relaying protocol itself.

Composability of the QKD interface.
In this work we aim to prevent Dependent-Key attacks
within one
AKE protocol.
We emphasize, however, that attacks on (concurrently)
composed
protocols are not captured in game-based AKE models –
in other words, our security model does not guarantee secure composability.
Even if QKD is secure and the AKE protocol is secure,
this does not always mean that its combination via the ETSI 014 QKD interface (described in
Section

2.5
) is secure.

To illustrate the concern, consider the following Dependent-Key attack
between different protocols
.
Imagine a scenario where Alice runs some (secure) protocol
\Pi
,
where she uses a (secure) QKD key retrieved via “Get key”.
Bob runs two protocols,
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\prime}
, and in both he retrieves the QKD key via “Get key with key IDs”.
The adversary can take the key ID sent by Alice in protocol
\Pi
,
and deliver it to Bob in protocol
\Pi^{\prime}
.
If
\Pi^{\prime}
happens to be vulnerable, this may leak the QKD key to the adversary,
which could be sufficient to impersonate Bob in protocol
\Pi
.
Note that neither QKD nor
\Pi
need to be broken by the adversary for this attack.

For most PQC protocols this issue does not result in real-world attacks,
because proper operational security practices (such as using a key only for a single protocol and protocol-specific key derivation) ensure that a key is only used in a single protocol.
In a typical QKD setup, however, these methods are often not deployed or not available.

To protect against such attacks, some binding between the QKD key and the protocol (and session) in which it is used seems to be necessary, which might even require a change in the specification of the QKD interface.
It is essential future work to extend our model to capture attacks of this type in order to handle such composability issues.
